# Private Generative Bootstrap via Blocking

**arXiv ID**: 2608.02480v1
**Authors**: Jinwon Sohn, Veronika Ročková
**Published**: 2026-08-03
**Categories**: stat.ML, cs.LG, stat.ME
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2608.02480v1

## Abstract

With AI systems gaining more access to individuals' information, it is important to protect privacy when reporting statistical answers. Equally important is to privatize the reporting of uncertainty in such answers. To this end, we adopt a Bayesian likelihood-free framework and make simulation from the posterior private. In particular, we propose a new private instantiation of the Bayesian bootstrap using a blocking strategy. Rather than assigning idiosyncratic random weights to each individual, we randomly group individuals and assign a single weight to each group. By concealing individuals' contributions within a group, we fortify differential privacy gates. We harness amortized inference that decouples private learning from posterior sampling. A push-forward map from observation weights to posterior samples is learned privately by adding calibrated noise during training. Subsequent posterior draws require no additional privacy and computation budget. We call the resulting method the Private Generative Bayesian Bootstrap (PGBB). We establish a differential privacy guarantee, analyze convergence to the non-private blocked-bootstrap target, and quantify the discrepancy between the ordinary and blocked Bayesian-bootstrap posteriors. In addition, we derive data-free tuning of the block Dirichlet concentration parameter that restores posterior dispersion asymptotically. We also show a single fit of PGBB can support a family of loss-based decision rules simultaneously without additional privacy cost. In simulations and in applications to U.S. Census returns to schooling and U.S. natality birthweight quantiles, PGBB gives competitive private uncertainty quantification and improves over private Bayesian alternatives that require a specified data-generating model in common settings.

Private Generative Bootstrap via Blocking

## Private Generative Bootstrap via BlockingJinwon Sohn
Booth School of Business, University of Chicago
and
Veronika Ročková
Booth School of Business, University of ChicagoCorresponding author:Jinwon.Sohn@ChicagoBooth.edu.

## Abstract

With AI systems gaining more access to individuals’ information, it is important to protect privacy when reporting statistical answers. Equally important is to privatize the reporting of uncertainty in such answers. To this end, we adopt a Bayesian likelihood-free framework and make simulation from the posterior private. In particular, we propose a new private instantiation of the Bayesian bootstrap using a blocking strategy. Rather than assigning idiosyncratic random weights to each individual, we randomly group individuals and assign a single weight to each group. By concealing individuals’ contributions within a group, we fortify differential privacy gates. We harness amortized inference that decouples private learning from posterior sampling. A push-forward map from observation weights to posterior samples is learned privately by adding calibrated noise during training. Subsequent posterior draws require no additional privacy and computation budget. We call the resulting method the Private Generative Bayesian Bootstrap (PGBB). We establish a differential privacy guarantee, analyze convergence to the non-private blocked-bootstrap target, and quantify the discrepancy between the ordinary and blocked Bayesian-bootstrap posteriors. In addition, we derive data-free tuning of the block Dirichlet concentration parameter that restores posterior dispersion asymptotically. We also show a single fit of PGBB can support a family of loss-based decision rules simultaneously without additional privacy cost. In simulations and in applications to U.S. Census returns to schooling and U.S. natality birthweight quantiles, PGBB gives competitive private uncertainty quantification and improves over private Bayesian alternatives that require a specified data-generating model in common settings.

Keywords:Amortized Inference; Differential Privacy; Loss-likelihood Inference; Nonparametric Inference; Private Uncertainty Quantification

## 1Introduction

Protecting individuals’ privacy while reporting statistical answers is an important consideration in modern data analysis. Differential privacy (DP), proposed byDworket al.(2006b), has become a standard mathematical formalism for such guarantees. This framework controls the effect of any single record on the released output even against adversaries with auxiliary information. Beyond point summaries, a private analysis must also quantify how uncertain those summaries are, all the more so because the injected privacy noise is itself an additional source of variability. We call the task of reporting statistically valid uncertainty under a formal privacy guaranteeprivate uncertainty quantification(private UQ), a goal targeted by a growing literature(Wanget al.2015, Ferrandoet al.2022, Avella-Medinaet al.2023, Awan and Wang2025, Wanget al.2025). Private UQ nonetheless remains challenging when one wishes to report statistically and practically reasonable uncertainty estimates without committing to a fully specified likelihood or data-generating process.

There have been various approaches for private UQ. Related to our work, a Bayesian formulation is natural for UQ because the posterior directly encodes uncertainty about the target, and a substantial literature has studied Bayesian inference under DP. Categorized mainly into three branches are privatizing posterior sampling itself(Wanget al.2015, Dimitrakakiset al.2017, Jewsonet al.2023), perturbing MCMC transitions or gradients(Heikkiläet al.2019, Liet al.2019, Zhang and Zhang2023), and releasing noisy summary statistics for subsequent Bayesian updating such as Gibbs sampling(Bernstein and Sheldon2018)or simulation-based draws(Gong2022, Juet al.2022). However, private UQ under standard Bayesian inference is compelling only when a working likelihood matches an underlying data-generating model. It is well-known that, under misspecification, the posterior may concentrate around a pseudo-true parameter and report credible sets calibrated to the wrong model(Müller2013). While the nonparametric private bootstrap(Wanget al.2025)avoids likelihood specification, it repeatedly accesses the data as many times as needed to collect private bootstrap estimates. This would require nontrivial privacy budgets in practice when numerous bootstrap samples are required. For these reasons, this work aims to develop a private Bayesian bootstrap method that directly overcomes the limitations of existing branches.

As a backbone inferential framework, we adopt a decision-theoretic perspective that defines the target as a functional of the population distribution. Namely, forX∼FX\sim Fand a lossl​(θ,x):Θ×𝒳→ℝ+l(\theta,x):\Theta\times{\cal X}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}, the inferential target isθ​(F):=arg​minθ∈Θ​∫l​(θ,x)​𝑑F​(x)\theta(F):=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\theta\in\Theta}\int l(\theta,x)\,dF(x), so the parameter is a decision rule rather than a component of a parametric model(Lyddonet al.2019, Fonget al.2019). Uncertainty aboutθ​(F)\theta(F)can be propagated from a nonparametric posterior onFFthrough the bootstrap. For instance, the posterior bootstrap ofFonget al.(2019)places a Dirichlet-process priorF∼𝒟​𝒫​(α,Fbase)F\sim{\cal DP}(\alpha,F_{\text{base}})on the data𝐗n=(X1,…,Xn){\bf X}_{n}=(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})and repeatedly re-solves the weighted optimization to obtain draws ofθ​(𝐰)\theta({\bf w})for𝐰∼Dirichlet​(α+n,αα+n​Fbase+nn+α​∑i=1nδXi){\bf w}\sim\text{Dirichlet}(\alpha+n,\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+n}F_{\text{base}}+\frac{n}{n+\alpha}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}}), reducing to Rubin’s Bayesian bootstrap(Rubin1981)whenα=0\alpha=0. The bootstrap is thus a natural vehicle that avoids specifying a working likelihood or data-generating process, toward private UQ nonparametrically. A recent advancement of Bayesian bootstrap(Nie and Ročková2023, Shinet al.2024)finds a generative map between𝐰{\bf w}andθ​(𝐰)\theta({\bf w}), known to enjoy computational scalability and produce effective i.i.d. posterior samples by construction.

Addressing the major limitations of existing work, this work suggests finding a private generative map under a block bootstrap scheme. To make this concrete, recall that the Bayesian bootstrap(Rubin1981)draws random weights𝐰=(w1,…,wn)∼Dirichlet​(𝟏n){\bf w}=(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})\sim\text{Dirichlet}(\mathbf{1}_{n})over thennrecords and reportsθ​(𝐰)\theta({\bf w}). Our construction, however, begins to replace these per-record Dirichlet weights by block-level Dirichlet weights𝐮=(u1,…,uS)∼Dirichlet​(γ​𝟏S){\bf u}=(u_{1},\dots,u_{S})\sim\text{Dirichlet}(\gamma\mathbf{1}_{S})wherennindividuals are randomly partitioned intoSSequal-sized blocks. In this design, an individual contribution to the optimization reduces from 1 to1/nS1/n_{S}in the worst case by concealing an individual into a subgroup, and therefore the block scheme provides a better privacy-preserving environment for devising an efficient private mechanism than the original scheme. Then, a proposed noisy training finds an amortized map which is the push-forward map from the blocked Dirichlet weights onto the block bootstrap posterior. We call the resulting map the Private Generative Bayesian Bootstrap (PGBB). Once PGBB is trained, any number of posterior draws is obtained by evaluating it at fresh weights, which is post-processing and spends no additional budget, in contrast to private MCMC(Heikkiläet al.2019, Zhang and Zhang2023)and private bootstrap(Wanget al.2025), whose budget grows with the number of draws to be released. Besides, PGBB can carry out private UQ for a range of decision rules without paying more privacy budgets, through joint amortized learning proposed inShinet al.(2024), which is an inherent advantage of PGBB. TableS1in SM contrasts PGBB with representative private inference methods. To our knowledge, this is the first work to harness the block bootstrap structure for private UQ.

The blocked construction for privacy introduces two statistical questions. First, larger blocks improve privacy protection, but they also make the blocked posterior bootstrap under-dispersed relative to the ordinary posterior bootstrap.Thus, blocking creates a tension between privacy and posterior fidelity. To address this, we calibrate the concentration parameterγ\gammaof the blocked Dirichlet weights so that the posterior dispersion is restored asymptotically, and notably, it does not require additional privacy budget. Second, because the posterior sampler is learned through noisy private training rather than solved exactly, understanding the gap between the private generator and the blocked bootstrap target is statistically important for understanding how privacy noise affects posterior approximation.

Our contributions are as follows. First, we develop the PGBB under a block-bootstrap scheme, with a DP guarantee that accounts for the randomness of block-level Dirichlet weights (Section2). Second, we analyze the statistical error from noisy private training. Section3gives, to our knowledge, the first convergence guarantee for an amortized Bayesian bootstrap under noisy training, with the non-private generator as a special case. Third, we quantify and correct the posterior distortion caused by blocking. Section4gives a finite-sample Wasserstein bound against the ordinary Bayesian bootstrap and introduces a concentration calibration that improves under-dispersion. Finally, Sections5and6show that PGBB gives reliable private uncertainty under misspecification and that one trained generator can answer many quantile targets without additional privacy budget. Proofs and additional experiments are collected in the Supplementary Material (SM).

## 2Private Generative Bayesian Bootstrap

## 2.1Differential Privacy

Bayesian inference may leak individuals’ privacy. Imagine posterior summaries of a target ruleθ\thetaare reported. An adversary may attempt to identify someone in the report or their private data record for potential exploitation by contrasting the institution’s statistics (or decision) with their own posterior summaries obtained from auxiliary information. In such a case, differential privacy (DP) shall ask the reported posterior quantities to satisfy an indistinguishability condition defined as follows.

## Definition 2.1(Dworket al.(2006a)).

Let𝐗n={Xi}i=1n{\bf X}_{n}=\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}and its neighboring database𝐗n′={Xi′}i=1n{\bf X}_{n}^{\prime}=\{X_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i=1}^{n}which differs in a single record, i.e.,Xi=Xi′X_{i}=X_{i}^{\prime}holds for alli≠ji\neq jexcept for a certainjj. For fixedϵ>0\epsilon>0andδ∈[0,1]\delta\in[0,1], a randomized mechanismℳ\mathcal{M}is called(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)-DP ifP​(ℳ​(𝐗n)∈ℬ)≤eϵ​P​(ℳ​(𝐗n′)∈ℬ)+δ,\displaystyle P(\mathcal{M}({\bf X}_{n})\in{\cal B})\leq e^{\epsilon}P(\mathcal{M}({\bf X}_{n}^{\prime})\in{\cal B})+\delta,(1)

for any neighboring databases𝐗n,𝐗n′{\bf X}_{n},{\bf X}_{n}^{\prime}and all Borel measurable setsℬ{\cal B}.

In words, the two posterior quantitiesℳ​(𝐗n)\mathcal{M}({\bf X}_{n})andℳ​(𝐗n′)\mathcal{M}({\bf X}_{n}^{\prime}), differing at only one arbitrary individual, are statistically indistinguishable up toeϵe^{\epsilon}tilting andδ\deltaprobability shift. We say(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)is the privacy budget. Asϵ,δ\epsilon,\deltaget smaller,ℳ\mathcal{M}has stronger privacy protection. A(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)-DP mechanism satisfies the following important property.

## Proposition 2.1(Post processing,Dworket al.(2014)).

Letℳ\mathcal{M}be an(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)-DP mechanism. For any measurable mapAA, the composed mechanismA∘ℳA\circ\mathcal{M}is also(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)-DP.

In our context, therefore, if the amortized generatorℳ{\mathcal{M}}is(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)-DP, then any downstream statistics constructed from the private sampler inherit the same(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)privacy level.

## 2.2Nonparametric Bayesian Bootstrap and Blocking

As established in the Introduction, our inferential target is the decision ruleθ​(F)=arg​minθ∈Θ​∫l​(θ,x)​𝑑F​(x)\theta(F)=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\theta\in\Theta}\int l(\theta,x)\,dF(x)forX∼FX\sim F, and we quantify uncertainty about it through the Bayesian bootstrap(Rubin1981), drawing weights𝐰=(w1,…,wn)∼Dirichlet​(𝟏n){\bf w}=(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})\sim\text{Dirichlet}(\mathbf{1}_{n})and reportsθDirichlet⋆:=θ⋆​(𝐰):=arg​minθ​∑i=1nwi​l​(θ,Xi)\theta^{\star}_{\text{Dirichlet}}:=\theta^{\star}({\bf w}):=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\,l(\theta,X_{i})for observationsX1,…,Xn∼FX_{1},\dots,X_{n}\sim Fi.i.d. This is theα=0\alpha=0instance of the posterior bootstrap ofFonget al.(2019), whose Dirichlet-process priorF∼𝒟​𝒫​(α,Fbase)F\sim{\cal DP}(\alpha,F_{\text{base}})additionally mixes the data with pseudo-samples from a priorFbaseF_{\text{base}}. We work throughout in the fully data-driven regimeα=0\alpha=0and defer the extension toα>0\alpha>0to Discussion. We next recall the amortized form of this bootstrap and then introduce blocking, the foundation of our privacy mechanism.

## 2.2.1Generative Bootstrap via Amortization

With remarkable computational efficiency, a recent amortized bootstrap method aims to find a generative map from the bootstrap weights to the estimates of the decision ruleθ​(F)\theta(F)(Shinet al.2021, Nie and Ročková2023, Shinet al.2024). Founded on the aforementioned nonparametric Bayesian framework, the amortized sampler is defined asG⋆:=arg​minG∈𝒢⁡𝔼𝐰∼Dirichlet​(𝟏n)​[∑i=1nwi​l​(G​(𝐰),Xi)],G​(𝐰):ℝn→ℝdθ,\displaystyle G^{\star}:=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{G\in{\cal G}}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bf w}\sim\text{Dirichlet}({\bf 1}_{n})}\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}l(G({\bf w}),X_{i})\Big],\quad G({\bf w}):\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d_{\theta}},(2)

wherel​(⋅,⋅):ℝdθ×𝒳→ℝ+l(\cdot,\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{d_{\theta}}\times{\cal X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}is a non-negative real-valued function. Intuitively, an ideal generator that attains the pointwise weighted minimum,G⋆​(𝐰):=arg​minθ​∑iwi​l​(θ,Xi)G^{\star}({\bf w}):=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\theta}\sum_{i}w_{i}l(\theta,X_{i})for almost every𝐰{\bf w}, satisfiesG⋆​(𝐰)=θDirichlet⋆​(𝐰)G^{\star}({\bf w})=\theta^{\star}_{\text{Dirichlet}}({\bf w})for a fresh draw𝐰{\bf w}(Shinet al.2024). Thus the idealG⋆G^{\star}serves as the push-forward map fromDirichlet​(𝟏n)\text{Dirichlet}({\bf 1}_{n})to the posterior distribution ofθDirichlet⋆\theta^{\star}_{\text{Dirichlet}}. In practice, a class𝒢NN={Gϕ:ϕ∈Φ}{\cal G}_{\text{NN}}=\{G_{\phi}:\phi\in\Phi\}of neural networks is employed whereΦ\Phiis the space of neural-net’s parameters. A feed-forward networkGϕ​(x)=WV​hV−1​(WV−1​hV−2​(⋯​W1​x))G_{\phi}(x)=W_{V}h_{V-1}(W_{V-1}h_{V-2}(\cdots W_{1}x))is a common example whereϕ=(W1,…,WV)\phi=(W_{1},\dots,W_{V})andh1,…,hV−1h_{1},\dots,h_{V-1}are nonlinear elementwise activation functions. The optimization (2) then simplifies to findingGϕ⋆​(𝐰):=arg​minϕ∈Φ⁡𝔼𝐰∼Dirichlet​(𝟏n)​[∑i=1nwi​l​(Gϕ​(𝐰),Xi)]G^{\star}_{\phi}({\bf w}):=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\phi\in\Phi}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bf w}\sim\text{Dirichlet}({\bf 1}_{n})}[\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}l(G_{\phi}({\bf w}),X_{i})]. Thus, for a fresh draw𝐰∼Dirichlet​(𝟏n){\bf w}\sim\text{Dirichlet}({\bf 1}_{n}),Gϕ⋆​(𝐰)G^{\star}_{\phi}({\bf w})approximatesG⋆​(𝐰)G^{\star}({\bf w})and accordinglyθDirichlet⋆​(𝐰)\theta^{\star}_{\text{Dirichlet}}({\bf w})as well. In particular, it is well-known that, ifG⋆G^{\star}is continuous, thenGϕG_{\phi}is a universal approximator; there existsGϕ∈𝒢NNG_{\phi}\in{\cal G}_{\text{NN}}such thatGϕG_{\phi}gets closer toG⋆G^{\star}asGϕG_{\phi}gets deeper and wider(Hanin2019). Posterior bootstrap samples are obtained by repeatedly drawing𝐰{\bf w}and evaluatingGϕ⋆​(𝐰)G^{\star}_{\phi}({\bf w}), and this work harnesses this decoupled inference from learning the stage for the privacy purpose remarked below.

## Remark 2.1.

Due to the decoupled phases between finding a posterior map and generating posterior samples from it, once the generative map is(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)-DP, the generation phase is also(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)-DP by the post-processing property (Proposition2.1).

The neural-net generatorGϕ⋆G^{\star}_{\phi}is estimated by the iterative update ofϕ\phias follows. Afterϕ(0)\phi^{(0)}is initialized, at iterationtt, we(i)sample𝐰t(k)∼Dirichlet​(𝟏n){\bf w}_{t}^{(k)}\sim\text{Dirichlet}({\bf 1}_{n})and construct an independent minibatchBt(k)B_{t}^{(k)}fork=1,…,Kk=1,\dots,K, and(ii)updateϕ(t+1)=ϕ(t)−η​∇gt\phi^{(t+1)}=\phi^{(t)}-\eta\nabla g_{t}where∇gt=1K​∑k=1K∑i∈Bt(k)wi,t(k)​∇l​(gt(k),Xi)|gt(k)=Gϕ(t)​(𝐰t(k))⋅∇ϕGϕ(t)​(𝐰t(k))\displaystyle\nabla g_{t}=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i\in B_{t}^{(k)}}w_{i,t}^{(k)}\nabla l(g^{(k)}_{t},X_{i})|_{g^{(k)}_{t}=G_{\phi^{(t)}}({\bf w}^{(k)}_{t})}\cdot\nabla_{\phi}G_{\phi^{(t)}}({\bf w}^{(k)}_{t})(3)

fort=1,…,Tt=1,\dots,T. Whennnis huge, the minibatch scheme brings substantial scalability. The averaging across1,…,K1,\dots,Kstands for the Monte Carlo estimate of the gradient since the target optimization (2) is defined as the average on the Dirichlet distribution. The last iterateGϕ(T)G_{\phi^{(T)}}is usually employed as an estimate ofGϕ⋆G^{\star}_{\phi}.

## 2.2.2Blocked Bootstrap to Curb Individual’s Contribution

Denote byπ:[n]→[S]\pi:[n]\to[S]a blocking process, where[N]={1,…,N}[N]=\{1,\dots,N\}. It partitions thennobservations intoSSdisjoint groupsI1,…,ISI_{1},\dots,I_{S}of equal sizenS:=n/Sn_{S}:=n/Sby sampling without replacement. AssumenS∈ℕn_{S}\in\mathbb{N}for simplicity. The blocked bootstrap collects the solutions ofθBlock⋆:=θ⋆​(𝐰¯):=arg⁡minθ​∑i=1nw¯i​l​(θ,Xi)\theta^{\star}_{\text{Block}}:=\theta^{\star}(\bar{\bf w}):=\arg\min_{\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{w}_{i}\,l(\theta,X_{i}), where𝐰¯=(w¯1,…,w¯n)\bar{\bf w}=(\bar{w}_{1},\dots,\bar{w}_{n})andw¯i=uπ​(i)/nS\bar{w}_{i}=u_{\pi(i)}/n_{S}, by drawing𝐮:=(u1,…,uS)∼Dirichlet​(γ​𝟏S){\bf u}:=(u_{1},\dots,u_{S})\sim\text{Dirichlet}(\gamma\mathbf{1}_{S})forγ>0\gamma>0. The blocking scheme underlies∑i=1nw¯i​δXi=∑s=1Sus​F^s\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{w}_{i}\delta_{X_{i}}=\sum_{s=1}^{S}u_{s}\hat{F}_{s}withF^s:=1nS​∑i∈IsδXi\hat{F}_{s}:=\tfrac{1}{n_{S}}\sum_{i\in I_{s}}\delta_{X_{i}}for a givenπ\pi. In other words, it can be a structured prior onFFthat puts a random Dirichlet mass on each block and an equal deterministic weight within it. Thus, the block bootstrap can be an exact posterior sampler when the estimand depends on the data only through the block aggregates{F^s}\{\hat{F}_{s}\}, and an approximation otherwise.

In this work, the block bootstrap with the partitionπ\pirandomized independently to(𝐮,𝐗n)({\bf u},{\bf X}_{n})offers two foundational merits in designing our privacy mechanisms. Specifically, we use the uniform random partitionπ=πτ​(i)=⌈τ​(i)​S/n⌉\pi=\pi_{\tau}(i)=\lceil\tau(i)\,S/n\rceil, whereτ\tauis a uniformly random permutation of[n][n]independent of(𝐮,𝐗n)({\bf u},{\bf X}_{n}). First, whereas the original bootstrap with𝐰∼Dirichlet​(𝟏n){\bf w}\sim\text{Dirichlet}({\bf 1}_{n})lets an individual contribute up to11, the block scheme caps each record’s worst-case influence at mostw¯i=uπ​(i)/nS≤1/nS\bar{w}_{i}=u_{\pi(i)}/n_{S}\leq 1/n_{S}. In other words, concealing an individual under the block scheme becomes more efficient than the original one in designing a DP mechanism. Also, it advocates using equal-sized blocks since an unbalanced partition scheme may increase the risk of revealing an individual’s worst-case contribution. Second, drawingπ\pias a data-independent random partition makes the guarantee agnostic to the realized blocks. Ifℳπ\mathcal{M}_{\pi}is(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)-DP mechanism conditional on every fixed partitionπ\pi, marginalizing preserves the same DP guarantee, i.e.,P​(ℳ​(D)∈ℬ)=∫P​(ℳπ​(D)∈ℬ)​𝑑ν​(π)≤eϵ​P​(ℳ​(D′)∈ℬ)+δP(\mathcal{M}(D)\in{\cal B})=\int P(\mathcal{M}_{\pi}(D)\in{\cal B})\,d\nu(\pi)\leq e^{\epsilon}P(\mathcal{M}(D^{\prime})\in{\cal B})+\delta, so privacy holds regardless of which blocks are realized, e.g., even if a block, by chance, mirrors some structure in the data.

Applying the block bootstrap for privacy purposes is novel to our knowledge. In statistics, a block-bootstrap procedure has been used for diverse reasons: time-series bootstraps(Künsch1989)to capture a time-dependency effect across data instances and amortized learning(Shinet al.2021)to improve scalability under a large sample regime. While grouping data for devising a privacy mechanism is not new in a high-level view, e.g., sample-and-aggregate for point estimation(Nissimet al.2007), teacher ensembles in supervised learning(Papernotet al.2017), we first utilize the block-bootstrap scheme for private uncertainty quantification.

## 2.3Noisy Training

Blocking alone is not enough to achieve privacy. Capping each record’s weight at1/nS1/n_{S}limits any single record’s influence, but it does not by itself make a released bootstrap optimizer DP. As a counterexample, considerXi=0X_{i}=0fori<ni<nandXn=MX_{n}=Mfor a largeMM. Even thoughXnX_{n}carries only its group’s share of the weight, the blocked bootstrap solutionθ⋆​(𝐰¯)\theta^{\star}(\bar{\bf w}), and alsoθ⋆​(𝐰)\theta^{\star}({\bf w}), still shifts detectably withMM, so releasing it with no further randomization can reveal whether thennth record is present, violating the indistinguishability in Definition2.1. Formal(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)-DP therefore requires an additional step, and we inject Gaussian noise during training. The role of blocking thus is seen to reduce an individual’s contribution, so that less noise is needed to conceal the individual during training.

To make the amortized learning phase(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)-differentially private, we suggest perturbing the update procedure of (3) with Gaussian noise. At thettth iteration, for a given partition ruleπ\pi, the gradient descent takes1K​∑k=1K(∑i∈Bt(k)w¯i,t(k)​∇l​(gt(k),Xi)+Zt(k))⋅∇ϕGϕ(t)DP​(𝐮t(k)),gt(k)=Gϕ(t)DP​(𝐮t(k)),\displaystyle\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\Big(\sum_{i\in B_{t}^{(k)}}\bar{w}_{i,t}^{(k)}\nabla l(g^{(k)}_{t},X_{i})+Z_{t}^{(k)}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{\phi}G_{\phi^{(t)}}^{\text{DP}}({\bf u}_{t}^{(k)}),\quad g^{(k)}_{t}=G_{\phi^{(t)}}^{\text{DP}}({\bf u}_{t}^{(k)}),(4)

whereZt(k)∼N​(0,σ2​𝐈)Z_{t}^{(k)}\sim{\rm N}(0,\sigma^{2}{\bf I})and𝐮t(k)∼Dirichlet​(γ​𝟏S){\bf u}_{t}^{(k)}\sim\text{Dirichlet}(\gamma{\bf 1}_{S}), and each index inBt(k)B_{t}^{(k)}is randomly picked byqqprobability for everyt,kt,k. The superscript ofGϕDPG_{\phi}^{\text{DP}}indicatesGϕG_{\phi}is paired with the noisy training (4). The Gaussian noise is added to the weighted output-gradient statistic before multiplication by the generator’s gradient because the generator does not take data points directly. For this reason, this mechanism may be seen as structurally less vulnerable to the Gaussian noise when compared to a common setup in the literature of standard private stochastic gradient descent(DP-SGD, Abadiet al.2016). Algorithm1in SMS1.2describes the updating scheme in detail. Note the finalTthT^{\text{th}}iterate of the gradient-based update by (4) stands forGϕ(T)DPG_{\phi^{(T)}}^{\text{DP}}, and next section showsGϕ(T)DPG_{\phi^{(T)}}^{\text{DP}}satisfies(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)-DP. More precisely, the collection{ϕ(t)}t=1T\{\phi^{(t)}\}_{t=1}^{T}from𝐗n{\bf X}_{n}isℳ​(𝐗n)\mathcal{M}({\bf X}_{n}), so evaluation of𝐮{\bf u}throughGϕ(T)DPG_{\phi^{(T)}}^{\text{DP}}is DP by the post-processing.

The block structure contributes substantially to enhancing the privacy protection over non-block noisy training. The Dirichlet weights mainly influence the randomness in (4) on top of the Gaussian privacy noise. Addressing the Dirichlet randomness is particularly important. Intuitively, an individual’s influence in (4) can be quantified bysupD,D′,g‖∑i=1nw¯i​∇l​(g,xi)−∑i=1nw¯i​∇l​(g,xi′)‖=supx,x′,g‖w¯i​(∇l​(g,x)−∇l​(g,x′))‖\sup_{D,D^{\prime},g}\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{w}_{i}\nabla l(g,x_{i})-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{w}_{i}\nabla l(g,x_{i}^{\prime})\|=\sup_{x,x^{\prime},g}\|\bar{w}_{i}(\nabla l(g,x)-\nabla l(g,x^{\prime}))\|. If‖∇l​(⋅,⋅)‖≤C\|\nabla l(\cdot,\cdot)\|\leq Cis uniformly bounded, the magnitude is bounded by2​w¯i​C2\bar{w}_{i}C. Although the block strategy limitsw¯i≤1/nS\bar{w}_{i}\leq 1/n_{S}, quantifying an individual’s influence by this deterministic upper bound, as a nominal DP-SGD application, may still be too conservative. Under the blocked Dirichlet weights, the marginal distribution satisfiesnS​w¯i∼Beta​(γ,(S−1)​γ)n_{S}\bar{w}_{i}\sim\mathrm{Beta}(\gamma,(S-1)\gamma), and most of its distributional mass is away from the endpoint11. Accounting for the Dirichlet randomness, therefore, can substantially reduce the individual contribution measured in privacy accounting.

## 2.4Certified Privacy for Noisy Training with Block

This section certifies that the noisy training of Section2.3produces an amortized generator satisfying(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)-DP, and characterizes the privacy gain from the Dirichlet randomness. For better readability, we first present the asymptotic analysis on the size of the privacy noiseσ\sigmain Theorem2.1, and then describe the formal(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)-DP guarantee in Proposition2.2.

## Theorem 2.1.

LetGϕ(T)DPG^{\text{DP}}_{\phi^{(T)}}be theTthT^{\text{th}}iterate of (4). Supposesupx,g‖∇l​(g,x)‖≤C\sup_{x,g}\|\nabla l(g,x)\|\leq Cfor someC>0C>0, and fixϵ>0\epsilon>0,T,K,S∈ℕT,K,S\in\mathbb{N},γ>0\gamma>0,q∈(0,1)q\in(0,1), and a targetδ∈(0,1)\delta\in(0,1). SetκS,γ:=γ+1S​(S​γ+1)\kappa_{S,\gamma}:=\tfrac{\gamma+1}{S(S\gamma+1)}. Assumelog⁡(1/δ)2​T​K​κS,γ≪1\sqrt{\tfrac{\log(1/\delta)}{2TK\kappa_{S,\gamma}}}\ll 1andq​log⁡(1/δ)(1−q)​ϵ=O​(1)\tfrac{q\log(1/\delta)}{(1-q)\epsilon}=O(1). At leading order, a sufficient condition forGϕ(T)DPG^{\text{DP}}_{\phi^{(T)}}to satisfy(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)-DP isσ\displaystyle\sigma≳C​qnS​T​K​log⁡(1/δ)​κS,γϵ,2​T​K​q2​C2​κS,γσ2​nS2≤log⁡(1/δ),Cσ​nS=o​(1).\displaystyle\gtrsim\frac{Cq}{n_{S}}\frac{\sqrt{TK\log(1/\delta)\kappa_{S,\gamma}}}{\epsilon},\qquad\frac{2TKq^{2}C^{2}\kappa_{S,\gamma}}{\sigma^{2}n_{S}^{2}}\leq\log(1/\delta),\qquad\frac{C}{\sigma n_{S}}=o(1).(5)

Equation (5) shows how the block sizenSn_{S}enters the privacy condition. The direct role of a largernSn_{S}is in the third termC/(σ​nS)=o​(1)C/(\sigma n_{S})=o(1), while a smallerSSincreasesκS,γ\kappa_{S,\gamma}and thereby makes the external regimeT​K​κS,γ≫log⁡(1/δ)TK\kappa_{S,\gamma}\gg\log(1/\delta)easier to satisfy. The noise scaleσ\sigmacan be negligible for a large-sample regime, but still increases byT​K\sqrt{TK}. ForT\sqrt{T}, the 1/2-rate aligns with the standard DP-SGD literature(Abadiet al.2016). AlthoughK\sqrt{K}appears to increase the required noise scale, its effect on the update is mitigated by the averaging overKKMonte Carlo draws. This point is made more explicit in the convergence analysis. The following remark characterizes the privacy amplification effect by encompassing the Dirichlet randomness, in contrast to the standard private SGD. SMS2.3.1writes the derivation.

## Remark 2.2.

For the Dirichlet-aware accountant, the leading noise scale is of orderC​q​T​K​log⁡(1/δ)​κS,γ/(nS​ϵ)Cq\sqrt{TK\,\log(1/\delta)\kappa_{S,\gamma}}/(n_{S}\epsilon)whereκS,γ=γ+1S​(S​γ+1)<1\kappa_{S,\gamma}=\tfrac{\gamma+1}{S(S\gamma+1)}<1forS≥2S\geq 2. If one ignores the Dirichlet distribution and uses only the deterministic boundw¯≤1/nS\bar{w}\leq 1/n_{S}, the corresponding worst-case scale is of orderC​q​T​K​log⁡(1/δ)/(nS​ϵ)Cq\sqrt{TK\log(1/\delta)}/(n_{S}\epsilon). Thus the ratio of the two leading scales isκS,γ\sqrt{\kappa_{S,\gamma}}when the validity conditions for each method are met.

The assumptionsupx,g‖∇l​(g,x)‖≤C\sup_{x,g}\|\nabla l(g,x)\|\leq Cis common in DP statistical estimation. A representative example is a private robust M-estimation(Avella-Medinaet al.2023). A wide range of statistical problems satisfies the uniform bound under realistic situations. For example, for the absolute lossl​(g;(y,x))=|y−x⊤​g|l(g;(y,x))=|y-x^{\top}g|, a subgradient with respect toggis−sign​(y−x⊤​g)​x-\mathrm{sign}(y-x^{\top}g)x, whose norm is bounded whenxxlies in a bounded domain. Likewise, on the bounded input domain, the pinball loss for quantile regression(Koenker and Bassett Jr1978)has the bounded derivative, and the Huber loss for robust regression does as well(Huber1992). If the condition is not met, one can enforce it by clipping only the multiplier∇l​(Xi,g)\nabla l(X_{i},g)in (4) before multiplying by∇ϕGϕDP​(𝐮)\nabla_{\phi}G_{\phi}^{\rm DP}({\bf u}).

Proposition2.2provides the formal(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)-DP guarantee of the noisy training by (4). One subtle point of our analysis is that neighboring databases𝐗n,𝐗n′{\bf X}_{n},{\bf X}_{n}^{\prime}are given the same Dirichlet distributions. In the DP-SGD literature,Birrellet al.(2024)presented the analysis of the noisy gradient with the Gaussian noise and Poisson subsampling under the replace-one neighboring scheme. Our analysis extends it to accommodate the Dirichlet randomness and the Monte Carlo averaging.

## Proposition 2.2.

LetAJ,λ,m​(q):=log⁡[1+q2​(MJ,λ+RJ,λ)+ℛJ,λ,m​(q)]A_{J,\lambda,m}(q):=\log[1+q^{2}(M_{J,\lambda}+R_{J,\lambda})+\mathcal{R}_{J,\lambda,m}(q)]whereℛJ,λ,m​(q)\mathcal{R}_{J,\lambda,m}(q)appears in (S32) of the SM. Then,Gϕ(T)DPG^{\text{DP}}_{\phi^{(T)}}satisfies(ϵ,δ⋆​(ϵ))(\epsilon,\delta_{\star}(\epsilon))-DP withδ⋆​(ϵ):=infλ∈ℕ,λ≥2exp⁡{T​K​AJ,λ,m​(q)−(λ−1)​ϵ}\delta_{\star}(\epsilon):=\inf_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N},\lambda\geq 2}\exp\bigl\{TKA_{J,\lambda,m}(q)-(\lambda-1)\epsilon\bigr\}.

Because the analysis tracks consumed privacy cost acrossTT, it is usually called privacy accounting. The same technique applies toℛJ,λ,m\mathcal{R}_{J,\lambda,m}. In this way, the formal privacy guarantee incorporates the randomness of the Dirichlet weights, leveraging the deterministic worst-case contribution. The choice ofJ,mJ,mdepends on the numerical stability, and we setJ=32J=32andm=5m=5by default. Figure1draws the pattern of the privacy curve in various cases. ## 2.5Joint Learning across Decision Rules

This section introduces a distinctive feature of the amortized sampler. A single generator can privatize the posterior of a range of decision rulesθτ\theta_{\tau}forτ∼Pτ\tau\sim P_{\tau}simultaneously under a given(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)privacy budget. More specifically, the amortized learning need not stick to a single decision rule. Letτ∈𝒯\tau\in\mathcal{T}index a family of losseslτ​(θ,x)l_{\tau}(\theta,x)with targetsθτ​(F):=arg​minθ∈Θ​∫lτ​(θ,x)​𝑑F​(x)\theta_{\tau}(F):=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\theta\in\Theta}\int l_{\tau}(\theta,x)\,dF(x). The index may encode a tuning parameter, a loss, or a scientific target, and in our quantile-regression application it is the quantile level. Following the multi-purpose sampler ofShinet al.(2024), we feed both the bootstrap weight and the index to one generator, and solveminG∈𝒢⁡𝔼𝐮∼Dirichlet​(γ​𝟏S),τ∼Pτ​[∑i=1nw¯i​lτ​(G​(𝐮,τ),Xi)]\min_{G\in\mathcal{G}}\ {\mathbb{E}}_{{\bf u}\sim\text{Dirichlet}(\gamma\mathbf{1}_{S}),\,\tau\sim P_{\tau}}\Big[\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{w}_{i}\,l_{\tau}(G({\bf u},\tau),X_{i})\Big]via the noisy training, extending of (4),1K​∑k=1K(∑i∈Bt(k)w¯i,t(k)​∇l​(gτ,t(k),Xi)+Zt(k))⋅∇ϕGϕDP​(𝐮t(k),τt(k)),gτ,t(k)=GϕDP​(𝐮t(k),τt(k)),\displaystyle\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\Big(\sum_{i\in B_{t}^{(k)}}\bar{w}_{i,t}^{(k)}\nabla l(g_{\tau,t}^{(k)},X_{i})+Z_{t}^{(k)}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{\phi}G_{\phi}^{\text{DP}}({\bf u}_{t}^{(k)},\tau_{t}^{(k)}),\quad g_{\tau,t}^{(k)}=G_{\phi}^{\text{DP}}({\bf u}_{t}^{(k)},\tau_{t}^{(k)}),

whereτt(k)∼Pτ\tau_{t}^{(k)}\sim P_{\tau}is independently sampled regardless of data. For DP guarantee, it suffices to set the uniform boundsupx,gτ‖∇l​(gτ,x)‖≤C\sup_{x,g_{\tau}}\|\nabla l(g_{\tau},x)\|\leq C. Once the private generatorGϕ(T)DP​(𝐮,τ)G^{\rm DP}_{\phi^{(T)}}({\bf u},\tau)is trained, evaluating it at anyτ\tauis post-processing, so draws and summaries across the entireτ∼Pτ\tau\sim P_{\tau}are tied to(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)budget. This brings a concrete privacy gain. In stark contrast, reporting private statistics over a grid ofτ\tauby running a separate private method perτ\tauaccesses the data repeatedly, so by composition, the budget grows according to the grid size. To our knowledge, this joint structure has not been developed (or explored) in existing private UQ literature.

## 3Convergence Analysis

Having established the privacy guarantee, this section studies how the privacy noise affects the training of the neural-net-based generatorGϕ(T)DPG^{\text{DP}}_{\phi^{(T)}}. We consider a standard feed-forward neural network with a bounded final activation. SetGϕ​(𝐮)=BG​hV​(G~ϕ​(𝐮))G_{\phi}({\bf u})=B_{G}h_{V}(\tilde{G}_{\phi}({\bf u})), whereG~ϕ​(𝐮):=WV​hV−1​(WV−1​hV−2​(⋯​W1​𝐮​⋯))\tilde{G}_{\phi}({\bf u}):=W_{V}h_{V-1}(W_{V-1}h_{V-2}(\cdots W_{1}{\bf u}\cdots))andϕ=(W1,…,WV)\phi=(W_{1},\dots,W_{V}).h1,…,hVh_{1},\dots,h_{V}are activation functions and supposehVh_{V}are bounded. Bias parameters are suppressed, since they can be represented by concatenating a constant11to the input. Output-clipping radiusBG>0B_{G}>0can be chosen so that[−BG,BG]dθ⊆Θ[-B_{G},B_{G}]^{d_{\theta}}\subseteq\Theta. This structural design allowsGϕ​(𝐮)∈ΘG_{\phi}({\bf u})\in\Thetafor anyϕ\phiand𝐮{\bf u}. We imposeϕ∈Φ:={ϕ:‖Wv‖op≤Bv,v=1,…,V}\phi\in\Phi:=\{\phi:\|W_{v}\|_{\text{op}}\leq B_{v},\;v=1,\dots,V\}and supposeh1,…,hVh_{1},\dots,h_{V}are twice differentiable.
This covers a wide range of activation functions, e.g., tanh, shifted-sigmoid, GELU, etc. The bounded weight matrices are common in theoretical analysis when neural networks are involved(Golowichet al.2018)and can also be readily satisfied via normalization or clipping techniques(Miyatoet al.2018).

Our analysis imposes the following conditions.(A1)

X1,…,XnX_{1},\dots,X_{n}are i.i.d. from a distributionFFsupported on a compact set𝒳⊂ℝdX\mathcal{X}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{X}}.(A2)

Θ⊂ℝdθ\Theta\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{\theta}}is convex and compact, and the population minimizerθ∞⋆:=arg⁡minθ∈Θ⁡𝔼​l​(θ,X)\theta_{\infty}^{\star}:=\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}{\mathbb{E}}\,l(\theta,X)is unique and lies inint​(Θ)\mathrm{int}(\Theta).(A3)

l​(x,θ)l(x,\theta)isC3C^{3}in(x,θ)(x,\theta)on a neighborhood of𝒳×Θ\mathcal{X}\times\Thetawith uniformly bounded derivatives up to order three.(A4)

l​(x,θ)l(x,\theta)is convex inθ\thetafor eachx∈𝒳x\in\mathcal{X}.(A5)

The population HessianH∞:=𝔼​[∇θ2l​(θ∞⋆,X)]H_{\infty}:={\mathbb{E}}[\nabla_{\theta}^{2}l(\theta_{\infty}^{\star},X)]satisfiesH∞⪰μ∞​𝐈H_{\infty}\succeq\mu_{\infty}{\bf I}for someμ∞>0\mu_{\infty}>0, andΣ∞:=𝔼​[∇l​(θ∞⋆,X)​∇l​(θ∞⋆,X)⊤]\Sigma_{\infty}:={\mathbb{E}}[\nabla l(\theta_{\infty}^{\star},X)\nabla l(\theta_{\infty}^{\star},X)^{\top}]denotes the population score second moment.

Together with the bounded network class, these assumptions imply uniform bounds on the generator Jacobian and Hessian. DefineL​(θ;𝐰):=∑i=1nwi​l​(θ,Xi)L(\theta;{\bf w}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}l(\theta,X_{i})for any given weight vector𝐰{\bf w}. Under (A1-5), sinceΦ\Phiis compact andGϕ​(𝐮)G_{\phi}({\bf u})is a finite composition of smooth activations and bounded weight maps, the generator’s Jacobian and parameter Hessian are uniformly bounded,β1:=supϕ∈Φ,𝐮‖Jϕ​(𝐮)‖op<∞\beta_{1}:=\sup_{\phi\in\Phi,\,{\bf u}}\|J_{\phi}({\bf u})\|_{\text{op}}<\inftyandβ2:=supϕ∈Φ,𝐮‖∇ϕ2Gϕ​(𝐮)‖op<∞\beta_{2}:=\sup_{\phi\in\Phi,\,{\bf u}}\|\nabla_{\phi}^{2}G_{\phi}({\bf u})\|_{\text{op}}<\infty. Our convergence analysis characterizes the discrepancy between the private amortized generatorGϕ(T)DP​(𝐮)G_{\phi^{(T)}}^{\text{DP}}({\bf u})and the exact blocked-bootstrap-solutionθBlock⋆\theta^{\star}_{\text{Block}}. Let𝝎T\boldsymbol{\omega}_{T}collect the algorithmic randomness used to obtain theTthT^{\text{th}}iterate, including initialization, minibatch sampling, Dirichlet draws used during training, and Gaussian perturbations.

## Theorem 3.1.

Suppose (A1-5) hold andϕt∈Φ\phi_{t}\in\Phiholds. LetℒΦ,𝐗n,π⋆:=infϕ∈Φℒ​(ϕ;𝐗n,π){\cal L}^{\star}_{\Phi,{\bf X}_{n},\pi}:=\inf_{\phi\in\Phi}\mathcal{L}(\phi;{\bf X}_{n},\pi)and suppose that there existsμℒ>0\mu_{\mathcal{L}}>0such that, for allϕ∈Φ\phi\in\Phi,‖∇ϕℒ​(ϕ;𝐗n,π)‖2≥2​μℒ​(ℒ​(ϕ;𝐗n,π)−ℒΦ,𝐗n,π⋆)\|\nabla_{\phi}\mathcal{L}(\phi;{\bf X}_{n},\pi)\|^{2}\geq 2\mu_{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{L}(\phi;{\bf X}_{n},\pi)-\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{\Phi,{\bf X}_{n},\pi})for every given𝐗n,π{\bf X}_{n},\pi. For fixedγ>0\gamma>0,q∈(0,1)q\in(0,1),η∈(0,1/(q​Mℒ)]\eta\in(0,\,1/(qM_{\mathcal{L}})], the last iterateGϕ(T)DPG_{\phi^{(T)}}^{\text{DP}}, for a fresh draw𝐮∼Dirichlet​(γ​𝟏S){\bf u}\sim\text{Dirichlet}(\gamma{\bf 1}_{S}), satisfies𝔼𝐗n,π,𝝎T,𝐮​‖Gϕ(T)DP​(𝐮)−θBlock⋆​(𝐰¯​(𝐮))‖2≲ℬT,\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bf X}_{n},\pi,\boldsymbol{\omega}_{T},{\bf u}}\bigl\|G^{\text{DP}}_{\phi^{(T)}}({\bf u})-\theta^{\star}_{\text{Block}}(\bar{\bf w}({\bf u}))\bigr\|^{2}\;\lesssim\;\mathcal{B}_{T},(6)

whereℬT=(1−η​q​μℒ)T​Δ0+Mℒ​ηq​μℒ​Πtotal+εapp+n−2\mathcal{B}_{T}={(1-\eta q\mu_{\mathcal{L}})^{T}\,\Delta_{0}}+{\frac{M_{\mathcal{L}}\,\eta}{q\,\mu_{\mathcal{L}}}\,\Pi_{\rm total}}+{\varepsilon_{\rm app}}+{n^{-2}}with initial gapΔ0:=𝔼𝐗n,π,ϕ0​[ℒ​(ϕ0;𝐗n,π)−ℒΦ,𝐗n,π⋆]\Delta_{0}:={\mathbb{E}}_{{\bf X}_{n},\pi,\phi_{0}}[\mathcal{L}(\phi_{0};{\bf X}_{n},\pi)-\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{\Phi,{\bf X}_{n},\pi}]and amortization gapεapp:=𝔼𝐗n,π​[ℒΦ,𝐗n,π⋆−𝔼𝐮​{L​(θBlock⋆​(𝐰¯​(𝐮));𝐰¯​(𝐮))}]\varepsilon_{\rm app}:={\mathbb{E}}_{{\bf X}_{n},\pi}[\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{\Phi,{\bf X}_{n},\pi}-{\mathbb{E}}_{\bf u}\{L(\theta^{\star}_{\text{Block}}(\bar{\bf w}({\bf u}));\bar{\bf w}({\bf u}))\}]. It splits into an optimization transient, a noise floor, an approximation error and a remainder, respectively. In particular,Πtotal=β12​C2​q​(1−q)​(γ+1)​SK​n​(S​γ+1)⏟Poisson subsampling+q2​β12​C2K⏟Monte Carlo+β12​dθ​σ2K⏟privacy noise.\displaystyle\Pi_{\rm total}=\underbrace{\frac{\beta_{1}^{2}C^{2}q(1-q)(\gamma+1)S}{Kn(S\gamma+1)}}_{\text{Poisson subsampling}}+\underbrace{\frac{q^{2}\beta_{1}^{2}C^{2}}{K}}_{\text{Monte Carlo}}+\underbrace{\frac{\beta_{1}^{2}d_{\theta}\sigma^{2}}{K}}_{\text{privacy noise}}.

The first term is a geometric transient that contracts at the per-iteration rate1−η​q​μℒ1-\eta q\mu_{\mathcal{L}}, so the initial optimization gap decays with the training horizonTT. The termεapp\varepsilon_{\rm app}is the generator-class approximation error, measuring the amortization gap between a single trained map and the pointwise bootstrap optimaθBlock⋆​(𝐰¯​(𝐮))\theta^{\star}_{\text{Block}}(\bar{\bf w}({\bf u})). This vanishes as the network classΦ\Phibecomes sufficiently expressive andΘ=[−BG,BG]dθ\Theta=[-B_{G},B_{G}]^{d_{\theta}}, as is natural under (A2).

The remaining termΠtotal\Pi_{\rm total}is the noise floor from the private update. It decomposes the effects of Poisson subsampling,KK-fold Monte Carlo averaging of the Dirichlet draws, and Gaussian privacy noise. IncreasingKKshrinks the Poisson and Monte Carlo summands, while the privacy term is effectivelyKK-invariant after substituting the leading-order noise scale from Theorem2.1. In this upper bound, the privacy term is then of smaller order because of itsn−2n^{-2}rate, although this should be read as an ordering within the bound rather than a tight comparison of the three randomness sources.

The existence of suchμℒ\mu_{\cal L}is the Polyak-Łojasiewicz (PL) condition. This condition is weaker than strong convexity and does not require convexity. It is commonly used to prove convergence for nonconvex objectives and over-parametrized models such as neural networks(Karimiet al.2016, Liuet al.2022). To our knowledge, this is the first convergence analysis for a neural-net-based amortized bootstrap generator. The theorem covers the non-private case by settingσ=0\sigma=0.

## 4Posterior Analysis of the Blocking Bootstrap

Previous sections show that the noisy training satisfies(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)-DP and that a larger block size can improve the privacy accounting. The statistical cost is that applying the blocked bootstrap to i.i.d. data may underestimate posterior uncertainty, since observations within a block share the same weight. This section studies this posterior distortion and how to correct it while keeping the privacy benefit.

## 4.1Posterior Gap Induced by Blocking

Letθ0⋆:=arg⁡minθ∈Θ⁡L​(θ;n−1​𝟏n)\theta_{0}^{\star}:=\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}L(\theta;n^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{n})be the empirical risk minimizer, whereL​(θ;𝐰):=∑i=1nwi​l​(θ,Xi)L(\theta;{\bf w}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}l(\theta,X_{i}). Recall that𝐮∼Dirichlet​(γ​𝟏S){\bf u}\sim\text{Dirichlet}(\gamma{\bf 1}_{S}),π\piis a uniform random partition, andw¯i=uπ​(i)/nS\bar{w}_{i}=u_{\pi(i)}/n_{S}. The blocked solution (blk-BB) isθBlock⋆​(𝐰¯):=arg⁡minθ⁡L​(θ;𝐰¯)\theta^{\star}_{\text{Block}}(\bar{\bf w}):=\arg\min_{\theta}L(\theta;\bar{\bf w}), while the ordinary Bayesian-bootstrap solution (org-BB) isθDirichlet⋆​(𝐰):=arg⁡minθ⁡L​(θ;𝐰)\theta^{\star}_{\text{Dirichlet}}({\bf w}):=\arg\min_{\theta}L(\theta;{\bf w})with𝐰∼Dirichlet​(𝟏n){\bf w}\sim\text{Dirichlet}(\mathbf{1}_{n}).

## Theorem 4.1.

Suppose (A1-5) hold with3≤S≤n3\leq S\leq n, andv∞𝐜:=𝐜⊤​H∞−1​Σ∞​H∞−1​𝐜>0v_{\infty}^{{\bf c}}:={\bf c}^{\top}H_{\infty}^{-1}\Sigma_{\infty}H_{\infty}^{-1}{\bf c}>0for a fixed contrast𝐜∈ℝdθ{\bf c}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{\theta}},‖𝐜‖=1\|{\bf c}\|=1. Supposeγ∈[γ¯,γ¯]⊂(0,∞)\gamma\in[\underline{\gamma},\overline{\gamma}]\subset(0,\infty)andS​γ≥3S\gamma\geq 3. Define the projected bootstrap lawsℒBlock𝐜,𝐗n,π:=Law​{𝐜⊤​n​(θBlock⋆​(𝐰¯)−θ0⋆)∣𝐗n,π}\mathcal{L}_{\text{Block}}^{{\bf c},{\bf X}_{n},\pi}:=\mathrm{Law}\{{\bf c}^{\top}\sqrt{n}\,(\theta^{\star}_{\text{Block}}(\bar{\bf w})-\theta_{0}^{\star})\mid{\bf X}_{n},\pi\}andℒDirichlet𝐜,𝐗n:=Law​{𝐜⊤​n​(θDirichlet⋆​(𝐰)−θ0⋆)∣𝐗n}\mathcal{L}_{\text{Dirichlet}}^{{\bf c},{\bf X}_{n}}:=\mathrm{Law}\{{\bf c}^{\top}\sqrt{n}\,(\theta^{\star}_{\text{Dirichlet}}({\bf w})-\theta_{0}^{\star})\mid{\bf X}_{n}\}. Then it follows that𝔼𝐗n,π​[W1​(ℒBlock𝐜,𝐗n,π,ℒDirichlet𝐜,𝐗n)]≲(S​γ+1)−1/2+n−1/2+|1−(S−1)​(n+1)(S​γ+1)​(n−1)|.\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bf X}_{n},\pi}\bigl[W_{1}\bigl(\mathcal{L}_{\text{Block}}^{{\bf c},{\bf X}_{n},\pi},\mathcal{L}_{\text{Dirichlet}}^{{\bf c},{\bf X}_{n}}\bigr)\bigr]\lesssim(S\gamma+1)^{-1/2}+n^{-1/2}+\bigl|1-\sqrt{\tfrac{(S-1)(n+1)}{(S\gamma+1)(n-1)}}\bigr|.(7)

We remark that standard bootstrap techniques requiring exchangeability(Præstgaard and Wellner1993, Cheng and Huang2010)do not directly apply because the blocked weights are non-exchangeable conditional onπ\pi.Shinet al.(2024)address this issue by randomizingπ\piand borrowing the standard consistency result withS≫nS\gg\sqrt{n}. In contrast, since privacy favors fixed and moderateSS, we compare the linearized blk-BB and org-BB through a finite normal-approximation bound(Chenet al.2010), which leaves the blocking error of orderS−1/2S^{-1/2}explicit. The third term in (7) stemming from the mismatch between each law’s variance disappears atγ=γ⋆​(n,S):=S​(n+1)−2​nS​(n−1).\displaystyle\gamma=\gamma^{\star}(n,S):=\frac{S(n+1)-2n}{S(n-1)}.(8)

Whenγ=γ⋆​(n,S)\gamma=\gamma^{\star}(n,S)is used in Theorem4.1,
the conditionS​γ≥3S\gamma\geq 3is guaranteed for alln>1n>1ifS≥5S\geq 5. After rescaling back byn−1/2n^{-1/2}, the same bound implies that the unscaled blocked and ordinary bootstrap laws become close for any fixedSS. Provided thatℒDirichlet𝐜,𝐗n\mathcal{L}_{\text{Dirichlet}}^{{\bf c},{\bf X}_{n}}is known to converge to a normal distribution(Lyddonet al.2019),θBlock⋆​(𝐰¯)\theta^{\star}_{\text{Block}}(\bar{\bf w})involves approximation error for frequentist coverage for fixedSSand improves by orderS−1/2S^{-1/2}.

The following proposition more specifically studies the implication ofγ⋆\gamma^{\star}in relating the first and second moments of the bootstrap solutions.

## Proposition 4.1.

Suppose (A1-5) hold with fixed3≤S≤n3\leq S\leq n. Then∥𝔼𝐗n,π[𝔼𝐮[θBlock⋆(𝐰¯)|𝐗n,π]−𝔼𝐰[θDirichlet⋆(𝐰)|𝐗n]]∥=O(n−1)\|{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bf X}_{n},\pi}[{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bf u}}[\theta^{\star}_{\text{Block}}(\bar{\bf w})|{\bf X}_{n},\pi]-{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bf w}}[\theta^{\star}_{\text{Dirichlet}}({\bf w})|{\bf X}_{n}]]\|=O(n^{-1})for any fixedγ∈[γ¯,γ¯]\gamma\in[\underline{\gamma},\overline{\gamma}]. Denote byVar(Z|𝒴):=𝔼[∥Z−𝔼[Z|𝒴]∥2|𝒴]\operatorname{Var}(Z|{\cal Y}):={\mathbb{E}}[\|Z-{\mathbb{E}}[Z|{\cal Y}]\|^{2}|{\cal Y}]the conditional variance ofZZgiven𝒴{\cal Y}. Atγ=γ⋆\gamma=\gamma^{\star}of (8), it follows|𝔼𝐗n,π​[Var⁡[θBlock⋆​(𝐰¯)∣𝐗n,π]]−𝔼𝐗n​[Var⁡[θDirichlet⋆​(𝐰)∣𝐗n]]|=O​(n−3/2)|{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bf X}_{n},\pi}[\operatorname{Var}[\theta^{\star}_{\text{Block}}(\bar{\bf w})|{\bf X}_{n},\pi]]-{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bf X}_{n}}[\operatorname{Var}[\theta^{\star}_{\text{Dirichlet}}({\bf w})|{\bf X}_{n}]]|=O(n^{-3/2}).

The calibrated concentration parameterγ⋆​(n,S)≤1\gamma^{\star}(n,S)\leq 1is an increasing function overSS. Intuitively, it diffuses the variability more for smallerSSso that the block weights and thus matches the posterior dispersion of blk-BB to that of org-BB. The valueγ⋆​(n,S)\gamma^{\star}(n,S)depends only on(n,S)(n,S)and not on the observed data𝐗n{\bf X}_{n}. Hence,this variance calibration is data-free and requires no additional privacy accounting. This is remarkably useful in the private setting. For instance, data-driven calibration methods, such as double-bootstrap corrections, would themselves have to be implemented under DP and composed with the rest of the analysis, which may require additional noise or privacy budget.

The ridge-type solution isθ⋆​(𝐰):=(X⊤​diag​(𝐰)​X+λ​I)−1​X⊤​diag​(𝐰)​y\theta^{\star}({\bf w}):=(X^{\!\top}\mathrm{diag}(\mathbf{w})X+\lambda I)^{-1}X^{\!\top}\mathrm{diag}(\mathbf{w})y. The right panel compares the frequentist coverage when eitherγ=1\gamma=1orγ=γ⋆\gamma=\gamma^{\star}is set. ## 4.2Role ofγ⋆\gamma^{\star}in Private Training

A natural follow-up question is then the role ofγ⋆\gamma^{\star}in noisy training. The convergence analysis Theorem3.1extends to answer this, and to this end, we suggestγ⋆\gamma^{\star}in the theorem is a conservative choice in noisy training. We make the dependence onγ\gammaexplicit by writingGϕ(T)(γ),DPG_{\phi^{(T)}}^{(\gamma),\text{DP}}and defineΔT​(γ):=𝔼𝐗n,π,𝝎T,𝐮​‖Gϕ(T)(γ),DP​(𝐮)−θBlock⋆​(𝐰¯​(𝐮))‖2\Delta_{T}(\gamma):={\mathbb{E}}_{{\bf X}_{n},\pi,\boldsymbol{\omega}_{T},{\bf u}}\|G_{\phi^{(T)}}^{(\gamma),\text{DP}}({\bf u})-\theta^{\star}_{\text{Block}}(\bar{\bf w}({\bf u}))\|^{2}. The following corollary extends Proposition4.1forGϕ(T)(γ),DPG_{\phi^{(T)}}^{(\gamma),\text{DP}}.

## Corollary 4.1.

Suppose the assumptions of Theorems3.1and4.1hold. LetaS,n​(γ)=(S−1)(S​γ+1)​(n−1)−1n+1a_{S,n}(\gamma)=\tfrac{(S-1)}{(S\gamma+1)(n-1)}-\tfrac{1}{n+1}. Then, it follows|𝔼𝐗n,π,𝛚T​Var𝐮⁡[Gϕ(T)(γ),DP​(𝐮)]−𝔼𝐗n​Var𝐰⁡[θDirichlet⋆​(𝐰)]|≤|aS,n​(γ)​an|+2​DΘ​ΔT​(γ)1/2+O​((1+S−1S​γ+1​γ−3/2)​n−3/2)|{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bf X}_{n},\pi,\boldsymbol{\omega}_{T}}\operatorname{Var}_{\bf u}[G_{\phi^{(T)}}^{(\gamma),\text{DP}}({\bf u})]-{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bf X}_{n}}\operatorname{Var}_{\bf w}[\theta^{\star}_{\text{Dirichlet}}({\bf w})]|\leq|a_{S,n}(\gamma)a_{n}|+2D_{\Theta}\Delta_{T}(\gamma)^{1/2}+O((1+\sqrt{\tfrac{S-1}{S\gamma+1}}\gamma^{-3/2})n^{-3/2})for some constantan≥0a_{n}\geq 0.

At the variance-matching concentrationγ⋆​(n,S)\gamma^{\star}(n,S)in Theorem4.1, the first leading term is|aS,n​(γ⋆)​an|=0|a_{S,n}(\gamma^{\star})a_{n}|=0exactly. Thus the trained generator approximates the original-bootstrap posterior variance up to its own training errorΔT​(γ⋆)1/2\Delta_{T}(\gamma^{\star})^{1/2}andO​(n−3/2)O(n^{-3/2}). Observe thataS,n​(γ)a_{S,n}(\gamma)is strictly decreasing w.r.t.γ\gamma. In the following remark, we observe thatγ⋆​(n,S)\gamma^{\star}(n,S)serves as a conservative choice in general.

## Remark 4.1.

Write the leading term in Corollary4.1asℛT​(γ)=|aS,n​(γ)|​an+ΨT​(γ){\cal R}_{T}(\gamma)=|a_{S,n}(\gamma)|a_{n}+\Psi_{T}(\gamma)withΨT​(γ):=2​DΘ​ΔT​(γ)1/2\Psi_{T}(\gamma):=2D_{\Theta}\Delta_{T}(\gamma)^{1/2}. AssumeΔT​(γ)\Delta_{T}(\gamma)is differentiable atγ⋆\gamma^{\star}. SinceaS,n​(γ⋆)=0a_{S,n}(\gamma^{\star})=0, the term|aS,n​(γ)||a_{S,n}(\gamma)|has a kink atγ⋆\gamma^{\star}with one-sided slopes±an​|aS,n′​(γ⋆)|\pm a_{n}|a^{\prime}_{S,n}(\gamma^{\star})|, wherean​|aS,n′​(γ⋆)|=S​(n−1)​an(S−1)​(n+1)2a_{n}|a^{\prime}_{S,n}(\gamma^{\star})|=\frac{S(n-1)a_{n}}{(S-1)(n+1)^{2}}, so the one-sided derivatives ofℛT{\cal R}_{T}atγ⋆\gamma^{\star}equal±an​|aS,n′​(γ⋆)|+ΨT′​(γ⋆)\pm a_{n}|a^{\prime}_{S,n}(\gamma^{\star})|+\Psi_{T}^{\prime}(\gamma^{\star}). Henceγ⋆\gamma^{\star}is locally optimal when|ΨT′​(γ⋆)|<an​|aS,n′​(γ⋆)||\Psi_{T}^{\prime}(\gamma^{\star})|<a_{n}|a^{\prime}_{S,n}(\gamma^{\star})|, whereasΨT′​(γ⋆)<−an​|aS,n′​(γ⋆)|\Psi_{T}^{\prime}(\gamma^{\star})<-a_{n}|a^{\prime}_{S,n}(\gamma^{\star})|admits a training-error-adjustedγ~⋆>γ⋆\tilde{\gamma}^{\star}>\gamma^{\star}withℛT​(γ~⋆)<ℛT​(γ⋆){\cal R}_{T}(\tilde{\gamma}^{\star})<{\cal R}_{T}(\gamma^{\star}). As expected,γ⋆<γ~⋆\gamma^{\star}<\tilde{\gamma}^{\star}would yield a larger uncertainty to the generator’s output and thus be regarded as conservative. In either caseγ⋆\gamma^{\star}removes the leading variance gap exactly and remains a principled data-free default.

The assumptionΨT′​(γ⋆)≤0\Psi_{T}^{\prime}(\gamma^{\star})\leq 0is realistic from our perspective. A largerγ\gammainjects less stochastic volatility to the training system thanγ⋆\gamma^{\star}, and thus the generator-error term is likely to stay or decrease forγ≥γ⋆\gamma\geq\gamma^{\star}. The remark is an effort to discuss thatγ⋆\gamma^{\star}can still serve as the principled default in noisy training. Estimatingγ~⋆\tilde{\gamma}^{\star}, however, seems practically intractable because it requires an additional privacy-accounting process, which may cost much more expensive budget than private training withγ⋆\gamma^{\star}.

## 5Simulation Studies for Private UQ

This section presents simulation studies to understand the statistical behavior of our neural-net-based private generative Bayesian bootstrap method (PGBB).
Our experiments consider two settings: likelihood misspecification in linear regression and well-specified exponential-family mean estimation. In all following experiments, PGBB employs a feed-forward network with three hidden layers, each of which has 256 nodes. The Adam optimizer with a learning rate decay executes the gradient descent procedure after perturbing the gradient. Since the optimizer operates on the already perturbed gradients, it essentially satisfies the post-processing and therefore does not affect the established privacy guarantee. PGBB returns 2k posterior samples. By default,γ=γ⋆\gamma=\gamma^{\star}for𝐮∼Dirichlet​(γ​𝟏S){\bf u}\sim\text{Dirichlet}(\gamma{\bf 1}_{S})is set. Other details of simulation studies appear in SMS3.

## 5.1Linear Regression under Heavy-tailed Errors

For each replicate, we drawn∈{10​k,40​k}n\in\{10\text{k},40\text{k}\}independent observations{(Xi,Yi)}i=1n\{(X_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}fromXi∼Uniform​[−1,1]pX_{i}\sim\mathrm{Uniform}[-1,1]^{p}andYi=Xi⊤​𝜷⋆+εiY_{i}=X_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\star}+\varepsilon_{i}with𝜷⋆=(1.5,−1.5,1.0)⊤\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\star}=(1.5,-1.5,1.0)^{\top}and noise scaleσε=0.5\sigma_{\varepsilon}=0.5. Two heavy-tailed error regimes are considered: Student-ttwithεi∼σε​t3\varepsilon_{i}\sim\sigma_{\varepsilon}\,t_{3}and contaminated normal withεi∼0.9​N​(0,σε2)+0.1​N​(0,64​σε2)\varepsilon_{i}\sim 0.9\,N(0,\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})+0.1\,N(0,64\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}). Both regimes are mean-zero, but represent a model misspecification setting if a likelihood-based Bayesian model assumes a standard normal distribution.

We compare PGBB with three private Bayesian or bootstrap baselines under the same target(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)-DP level, withδ=1/n\delta=1/nunless stated otherwise. PGBB and DP-Bootstrap directly use the Huber lossl​((β,σH),(Yi,Xi))=σH​ρc​((Yi−Xi⊤​β)/σH)+κ​σH/2l((\beta,\sigma_{H}),(Y_{i},X_{i}))=\sigma_{H}\rho_{c}((Y_{i}-X_{i}^{\top}\beta)/\sigma_{H})+\kappa\sigma_{H}/2, whereρc​(z)=z2/2\rho_{c}(z)=z^{2}/2for|z|≤c|z|\leq candρc​(z)=c​|z|−c2/2\rho_{c}(z)=c|z|-c^{2}/2otherwise. We setc=1.345c=1.345andκ=0.71\kappa=0.71, followingAvella-Medinaet al.(2023). NoisyGibbs(Bernstein and Sheldon2019)implements a Gibbs sampler under a Gaussian linear model with summary statistics perturbed, where the non-private statistics play as a latent variable. To compare the performance of private UQ, we customize a DP-SGLD baseline (DP-SGLD) that uses Poisson subsampling and Gaussian perturbation to the updating gradient, so that it provides multiple posterior samples under the same privacy accounting procedure as ours except for the Dirichlet randomness. Note that a last iterate of SGLD can be DP under regularity conditions(Liet al.2019, Bertazziet al.2025), but a single posterior draw is not enough for reasonable UQ. Our customized DP-SGLD is designed to use the same Huber loss via the Gibbs posterior(Bissiriet al.2016), i.e.,p​(β,σH|{Yi,Xi}i=1n)∝exp⁡(−ω​∑i=1nl​((β,σH),(Yi,Xi)))p(\beta,\sigma_{H}|\{Y_{i},X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n})\propto\exp(-\omega\sum_{i=1}^{n}l((\beta,\sigma_{H}),(Y_{i},X_{i}))). Notably, our bootstrap-based method does not introduce such a scaling factorω\omega.

For a fixed-draw comparison, PGBB releases 2k posterior draws afterT=10​kT=10\text{k}iterations. DP-SGLD runs20​k20\text{k}iterations to secure a moderate effective sample size (ESS) and uses half of its chain for inference. Similarly, NoisyGibbs runs400​k400\text{k}iterations, considering ESS, and returns half of the chain. See SM TableS7summarizing ESS for both methods. Notably, DP-Bootstrap and PGBB generate fully effective samples by construction. DP-Bootstrap releases2020private bootstrap samples. We avoid the trivial failure mode for each method. For instance, the performance of DP-SGLD and DP-Bootstrap tends to deteriorate as more private estimates are collected, while PGBB can generate an arbitrary number of posterior estimates without any privacy restriction. Likewise, using too many iterationsTTfor PGBB may result in an uninformative sampler.(a)Comparison of competing methods(b)Comparison ofγ=1\gamma=1andγ⋆​(n,S)\gamma^{\star}(n,S)atS∈{20,5}S\in\{20,5\}Figure 3:Linear regression under heavy-tailed errors. In panel (a), each point averages the metric over33coefficients within each replicate and over100100replicates; error bars for MAE and CI width show±2\pm 2Monte Carlo SEs across replicate-level averages. Panel (b) comparesγ=1\gamma=1andγ⋆\gamma^{\star}by coverage and CI width.

## Results

Figure3(a)overlays the results. PGBB attains competitive MAE and the narrowest credible intervals in every combination ofn×ϵn\times\epsilon, while maintaining frequentist coverage close to the nominal95%95\%level. Performance overall improves asnnandϵ\epsilonincrease. PGBB targets the robust loss-based decision rule directly and therefore does not have to fit the contaminated data through a Gaussian likelihood. In contrast, the likelihood-based private Bayesian methods pay a cost for this mismatch, either through wider uncertainty or deteriorating coverage. NoisyGibbs makes this cost especially clear under contamination, as its empirical coverage deteriorates asnngrows due to the wrong Gaussian likelihood. DP-Bootstrap exposes a different failure mode. It is extremely sensitive to the choice ofℓ2\ell_{2}-regularizer (used for formal privacy guarantee) even when the number of posterior samples is scarce. Lastly, SMS1.3.2verifies theKK-invariance of PGBB’s estimation performance discussed in Theorem3.1. SM TableS2expands the numerical values of Figure3(a), and SM TablesS3–S5give coefficient-specific results.

## 5.1.1Choice ofγ\gamma

Figure3(b)contrasts the coverage onγ=1\gamma=1with the variance-matchedγ=γ⋆​(n,S)\gamma=\gamma^{\star}(n,S)of Proposition4.1. Since the MAE remains almost the same, the figure summarizes coverage and CI width only. The canonicalγ=1\gamma=1under-covers atS=5S=5, dipping to0.87∼0.880.87\sim 0.88at(ε,n)=(4,10​k)(\varepsilon,n)=(4,\,10\text{k})under both noise regimes, whereasγ=γ⋆\gamma=\gamma^{\star}recovers the nominal coverage level by slightly widening the credible intervals. Thus, whileγ=1\gamma=1can be a serviceable default at moderateSS, the calibrated choiceγ⋆\gamma^{\star}provides a simple correction when coverage is sensitive to blocking.

## 5.2Well-specified Likelihood

This section is designed to see whether the PGBB’s uncertainty quantification is comparable to a known noise-aware Bayesian method. We consider Gaussian-mean estimationXi∼𝒩​(1.5,0.52)X_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(1.5,0.5^{2})and exponential-rate estimationXi∼Exp​(1/2)X_{i}\sim\mathrm{Exp}(1/2)at relatively small sample sizesn∈{500,5000}n\in\{500,5000\}. The target estimand is𝔼​[X]{\mathbb{E}}[X]in both cases. For these exponential-family models, the noise-aware sufficient-statistic perturbation ofBernstein and Sheldon (2018), called NoisyGibbs, explicitly models the privacy noise in constructing the inference. Both DP-SGLD and PGBB use their clipped log likelihood. DP-Bootstrap reports 1k deconvoluted bootstrap estimates still far less than 2k, which is available only for a univariate parameter(Wanget al.2025). The total number of iterations of DP-SGLD and NoisyGibbs is determined to secure moderate ESS (SM TableS7). Not surprisingly, for DP-SGLD, longer chains require more noise per step, which can degrade the estimate. PGBB and DP-Bootstrap draw independent samples always. DP-Bootstrap, however, spends more privacy budget to collect more draws, while NoisyGibbs does not in collecting more samples.Figure 4:Exponential-family experiment. Points are means over100100replicates. Error bars, shown for MAE and CI width, give±2\pm 2Monte Carlo SEs across replicates. Coverage is the empirical inclusion rate of nominal95%95\%credible intervals.

Figure4demonstrates that PGBB is comparable when a model is correctly specified. All models have nominal coverages, although DP-SGLD has overly extensive coverages. NoisyGibbs tends to give relatively smaller MAE, coverage closer to the target95%95\%, and the shortest intervals. Interestingly, PGBB stays closer to the gold standard than competing methods, particularly for the case ofn=500n=500; MAE and interval width are slightly larger than those of NoisyGibbs, and coverage remains near nominal. While DP-Bootstrap underperforms in linear regression, it efficiently works in this exponential family example whenn=5​kn=5\text{k}. DP-SGLD achieves a smaller MAE in the Gaussian case, but it has substantially wider CI width.

## 6Real Data Application

Quantile regression is an effective tool for distributional-level analysis. For instance,Angristet al.(2006)fit quantile regression for a grid of quantiles to study how thereturn-to-educationvaries across thewagedistribution onU.S. Census data, where the response is the log weekly wage and the covariates are years of schooling, potential work experience, and a black indicator. They reported rising within-group inequality in the upper half of the distribution between 1980 (n=65,023n=65,023) and 2000 (n=97,397n=97,397). In another example,Abrevaya (2001)studied how maternal demographics and behavior shift the distribution of birthweight forU.S. Natality data. Fitting the quantile regression ofbirthweightfor various levels, the study found that several factors, e.g., notablymaternal-smoking,race,education, andprenatal-care, have a markedly larger effect at the lower quantiles.(a)Returns to schooling in 1980 and 2000.(b)Birthweight coefficients (grams) for no-smoking and black indicators.Figure 5:Released DP quantile-regression coefficients acrossτ\tau: PGBB and DP-SGLD (ω∈{1,0.1}\omega\in\{1,0.1\}) versus the non-private fit. Boxplots overlay three random runs per method.

The proposed joint PGBB scheme in Section2.5can be highly beneficial when protecting individuals across all quantile levels is desirable, like the two aforementioned applications. We apply the kernel-smoothed quantile lossρτ,h​(u)=(ρτ∗Kh)​(u)=∫−∞∞ρτ​(v)​Kh​(u−v)​dv\rho_{\tau,h}(u)=(\rho_{\tau}*K_{h})(u)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\rho_{\tau}(v)\,K_{h}(u-v)\,\mathrm{d}v(Tanet al.2022), whereKh​(⋅)K_{h}(\cdot)is a non-negative kernel of bandwidthhh, whose derivative lies in[τ−1,τ][\tau-1,\tau]and is therefore bounded in magnitude bymax⁡{τ,1−τ}\max\{\tau,\,1-\tau\}. Accordingly, DP-SGLD uses the per-τ\tauboundC​(τ)=max⁡{τ,1−τ}​BXC(\tau)=\max\{\tau,1-\tau\}B_{X}with‖X‖≤BX\|X\|\leq B_{X}and PGBB usessupτC​(τ)\sup_{\tau}C(\tau)for the joint training. We setτ∼discrete​({ok}k=15,{pk}k=15)\tau\sim\mathrm{discrete}(\{o_{k}\}_{k=1}^{5},\{p_{k}\}_{k=1}^{5})whereok=(2​k−1)/10o_{k}=(2k-1)/10andpk∝(ok​(1−ok))−1p_{k}\propto{(o_{k}(1-o_{k}))^{-1}}. In each example, the original non-private quantile regression and the customized DP-SGLD via the Gibbs posterior are provided as reference models. Because DP-SGLD runs over the 5 grids ofτ\tauindependently, the privacy accountant of DP-SGLD is updated to reflect this composition under the Rényi composition(Mironov2017)rather than using the loose linear composition. Two DP-SGLD models are fitted forω∈{1,0.1}\omega\in\{1,0.1\}forT=200​kT=200\text{k}; both have the average effective sample size across all variables still less than100100. To avoid trivial failure (increasingTTand perturbing gradients substantially more), we do not increaseTTfurther. ## Results

Figures5(a)and5(b)show the released coefficients acrossτ\tauwith boxplots over three runs. In both applications, the posterior mean of both methods tracks the non-private fit. In particular, PGBB returns only slightly wider95%95\%intervals, so the findings of non-private analysis are preserved. Intervals widen withmax⁡{τ,1−τ}\max\{\tau,1-\tau\}and for less precisely identified coefficients such as not-smoking, where the privacy noise dominates. Figure6more specifically assesses such phenomena in detail. It relates the standard error of non-private coefficients to within-variability and between-variability, respectively. As we observe, PGBB has smaller within-variability overall, but slightly larger between-variability. Over1010independent runs, Table1shows that PGBB has smaller MAE and moderate widths at comparable coverage, whereas DP-SGLD overall has the overcoverage issue and is sensitive to the choice ofω\omega. SMS1.3.4reports the remaining covariates and the pooled plots.Figure 6:Posterior variability versus the non-private standard errorSE^​(β^)\widehat{\mathrm{SE}}(\hat{\beta}), decomposed overR=10R=10independent runs and plotted on the standard-deviation scale. Panels show (a) within-run variability{R−1​∑rVar^b​(βr(b))}1/2\{R^{-1}\sum_{r}\widehat{\mathrm{Var}}_{b}(\beta_{r}^{(b)})\}^{1/2}, (b) between-run variability{Var^r​(β¯r)}1/2\{\widehat{\mathrm{Var}}_{r}(\bar{\beta}_{r})\}^{1/2}, and (c) total variability, the square root of the sum of the two variances. Panels pool all Census and Natality covariates atε=1\varepsilon=1across the quantile grid, with shared log axes.

## 7Discussion

This work develops a differentially private generative Bayesian bootstrap (PGBB). The method combines three advantages not jointly provided by existing private-UQ methods: decoupled private learning followed by post-processing, freedom from specifying a data-generating process, and joint learning for a range of decision rules under a single privacy budget. We believe these features can reduce the impact of privacy noise to improve uncertainty quantification in diverse real-world applications, such as when a range of loss-based decision rules with uncertainty is required for reliable inference, as in our quantile regression applications (Section6). Nevertheless, we acknowledge these promising properties can come at a computational cost, since fitting a large amortized generator may require a high-performance GPU.

Several practical extensions may further improve private amortized learning. Shuffling the partitionπ\piat each training iteration could help when a subgroup contains outliers and would preserve privacy, although the calibration ruleγ⋆\gamma^{\star}would no longer apply. Nested random reweighting within groups is another possibility, but an efficient formal privacy accountant may be nontrivial. Finally, allowing pseudo-samples withα>0\alpha>0in the posterior bootstrap is attractive for privacy (SMS2.3.2) and connects to the growing use of generative-AI outputs(O’Hagan and Ročková2025). We leave this to future work because posterior-variance calibration and bias control become more subtle.

## 9Data Availability Statement

The U.S. Census data and the U.S. natality data analyzed in this article are publicly available. The U.S. Census data are the public-use samples analyzed byAngristet al.(2006), and the natality records are distributed by the National Bureau of Economic Research athttps://www.nber.org/research/data/vital-statistics-natality-birth-data.

## S1.3.4Further Results of Real Data Application

TablesS8andS9extend Table1to all covariates. Across both applications the two methods attain comparable MAE, while PGBB produces the narrower credible interval for every coefficient. On the U.S. Census (TableS8), PGBB’s intervals are several times tighter than DP-SGLD’s at similar MAE. Its coverage, the inclusion rate of the non-private point estimate, is close to nominal forblackbut lower for the returns to schooling,educandexper, whose non-private signal is small relative to the privacy noise. DP-SGLD is much wider and over-covers throughout. FigureS1plots the released coefficients acrossτ\taufor all covariates and both years. On the natality data (TableS9), the same pattern holds. PGBB gives the tightest intervals for all thirteen covariates and near-nominal coverage for the well-identified ones such asBLACK,MARRIED, andBOY. Its coverage is lower forNOSMOKEandAGE_s, improving asϵ\epsilongrows, and lowest for the weakly identifiedCIGAR_s. DP-SGLD attains slightly smaller MAE on several coefficients but at two-to-three times the interval width, and it over-covers almost everywhere. FigureS2plots the released coefficients acrossτ\taufor all thirteen covariates.

The two datasets show the same trade-off. From a single quantile loss with no scale parameter, PGBB tends to give the smaller and better-calibrated intervals at MAE competitive with DP-SGLD. Its coverage is near nominal where the coefficient is well identified and lower where the non-private signal is small relative to the privacy noise. DP-SGLD is governed byω\omega. Lowering it toω=0.1\omega=0.1widens the intervals and raises coverage on the well-identified coefficients, but even atω=1\omega=1its intervals stay wider than PGBB’s while over-covering. The weakly identified coefficients, such asCIGAR_sandexper, carry little non-private signal, so the privacy noise dominates them.Table S8:Numerics for the U.S. Census returns-to-schooling application, overτ\tauand1010replicates, for all covariates by year. ## S1.4Conversion from RDP to DP

Proposition2.2presents(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)-DP for simplicity. Its derivation, however, exploits the Rényi differential privacy (RDP) that measures privacy by the Rényi divergence between the two output distributions on neighboring databases(Mironov2017). For an integer orderλ>1\lambda>1, theλ\lambda-Rényi divergence betweenPPandQQis defined asDλ​(P∥Q)=1λ−1​log⁡𝔼Q​[(d​P/d​Q)λ]D_{\lambda}(P\|Q)=\tfrac{1}{\lambda-1}\log{\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[(dP/dQ)^{\lambda}]. A mechanismℳ\mathcal{M}is(λ,γ​(λ))(\lambda,\gamma(\lambda))-RDP ifDλ​(ℳ​(D)∥ℳ​(D′))≤γ​(λ)D_{\lambda}(\mathcal{M}(D)\|\mathcal{M}(D^{\prime}))\leq\gamma(\lambda)for every replace-one neighboring pairD,D′D,D^{\prime}. Proof of Proposition2.2mainly uses the order-λ\lambdalog moment, i.e.,log⁡𝔼Q​[(d​P/d​Q)λ]\log{\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[(dP/dQ)^{\lambda}]and the RDP-to-DP conversion formula suggested inMironov (2017)to state(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)-DP. More specifically, Proposition2.2bounds the order-λ\lambdalog-moment of one iteration byAJ,λ,m​(q)A_{J,\lambda,m}(q), so the single release is(λ,AJ,λ,m​(q)/(λ−1))\bigl(\lambda,\,A_{J,\lambda,m}(q)/(\lambda-1)\bigr)-RDP. The generator runsT​KTKsuch releases, so the total RDP isγMtot​(λ)=T​K​AJ,λ,m​(q)/(λ−1)\gamma^{\mathrm{tot}}_{M}(\lambda)=TK\,A_{J,\lambda,m}(q)/(\lambda-1). The conversion formula ofMironov (2017)givesδ≤exp⁡{(λ−1)​(γ​(λ)−ϵ)}=exp⁡{T​K​AJ,λ,m​(q)−(λ−1)​ϵ}\delta\leq\exp\{(\lambda-1)(\gamma(\lambda)-\epsilon)\}=\exp\{TK\,A_{J,\lambda,m}(q)-(\lambda-1)\epsilon\}. Minimizing this over the integer ordersλ≥2\lambda\geq 2recoversδ⋆​(ϵ)\delta_{\star}(\epsilon)in Proposition2.2. Henceδ⋆​(ϵ)\delta_{\star}(\epsilon)is simply the total Rényi cost over theT​KTKsteps, converted to(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)and tightened over the orderλ\lambda.

The Mironov bound underlyingδ⋆​(ϵ)\delta_{\star}(\epsilon)is transparent but not tight enough. Lemma 1 inAsoodehet al.(2021)characterizes a more efficient conversion formula by adopting an optimization perspective on the relationship between DP and RDP. It applies to any(λ,γ​(λ))(\lambda,\gamma(\lambda))-RDP mechanism at a fixed orderλ>1\lambda>1. Withζλ:=(λ−1)λ−1/λλ\zeta_{\lambda}:=(\lambda-1)^{\lambda-1}/\lambda^{\lambda}and a targetδ∈(0,1)\delta\in(0,1), ifλ​δ≤1\lambda\delta\leq 1thenℳ\mathcal{M}is(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)-DP wheneverϵ≥1λ−1​min⁡{[(λ−1)​γ​(λ)−log⁡(δ/ζλ)]+,log⁡(1+e(λ−1)​γ​(λ)−1λ​δ)},\displaystyle\epsilon\geq\frac{1}{\lambda-1}\min\Bigl\{\bigl[(\lambda-1)\gamma(\lambda)-\log(\delta/\zeta_{\lambda})\bigr]_{+},\log\Bigl(1+\tfrac{e^{(\lambda-1)\gamma(\lambda)}-1}{\lambda\delta}\Bigr)\Bigr\},(S1)

and ifλ​δ≥1\lambda\delta\geq 1it suffices thatϵ≥(γ​(λ)+log⁡(1−δ))+\epsilon\geq\bigl(\gamma(\lambda)+\log(1-\delta)\bigr)_{+}. We substituteγ​(λ)=γMtot​(λ)\gamma(\lambda)=\gamma^{\mathrm{tot}}_{M}(\lambda)from SectionS2.2.3and minimize over the integer ordersλ≥2\lambda\geq 2. This also satisfies the(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)guarantee, and it is never weaker than(ϵ,δ⋆​(ϵ))(\epsilon,\delta_{\star}(\epsilon)). We therefore state Proposition2.2in the transparent form for better readability and leverage this sharper conversion for simulation.

## S1.5Bisection Scheme to Select the Noise Scaleσ\sigma

In the simulation, the noise scaleσ\sigmais determined by the target privacy budget(ϵ,δ)(\epsilon,\delta)and other hyperparameters(n,S,α,q,C,T,K)(n,S,\alpha,q,C,T,K). The per-iteration log-momentAJ,λ,m​(q)A_{J,\lambda,m}(q)depends onσ\sigmaonly through the ratioC/σC/\sigma, which entersMJ,λM_{J,\lambda},RJ,λR_{J,\lambda}, and the higher-order remainderℛJ,λ,m​(q)\mathcal{R}_{J,\lambda,m}(q), so the total RDPγMtot​(λ)=T​K​AJ,λ,m​(q)/(λ−1)\gamma^{\mathrm{tot}}_{M}(\lambda)=TK\,A_{J,\lambda,m}(q)/(\lambda-1)is a function ofσ\sigma. At the fixed targetδ\delta, the conversion (S1) turns this RDP into a privacy level at each order, and the tightest one over the integer orders isϵ​(σ)=minλ≥2⁡ϵλδ​(γMtot​(λ)),\displaystyle\epsilon(\sigma)=\min_{\lambda\geq 2}\ \epsilon^{\delta}_{\lambda}\bigl(\gamma^{\mathrm{tot}}_{M}(\lambda)\bigr),

whereϵλδ​(γ)\epsilon^{\delta}_{\lambda}(\gamma)is the smallestϵ\epsilonthat (S1) admits at orderλ\lambda, that is,ϵλδ​(γ)={1λ−1​min⁡{[(λ−1)​γ−log⁡(δ/ζλ)]+,log⁡(1+e(λ−1)​γ−1λ​δ)},λ​δ<1,(γ+log⁡(1−δ))+,λ​δ≥1.\displaystyle\epsilon^{\delta}_{\lambda}(\gamma)=\begin{cases}\dfrac{1}{\lambda-1}\min\Bigl\{\bigl[(\lambda-1)\gamma-\log(\delta/\zeta_{\lambda})\bigr]_{+},\ \log\Bigl(1+\tfrac{e^{(\lambda-1)\gamma}-1}{\lambda\delta}\Bigr)\Bigr\},&\lambda\delta<1,\\[6.0pt]
\bigl(\gamma+\log(1-\delta)\bigr)_{+},&\lambda\delta\geq 1.\end{cases}

We then solve for the smallest noise that meets the target,σ⋆=min⁡{σ>0:ϵ​(σ)≤ϵ}.\displaystyle\sigma^{\star}=\min\{\sigma>0:\ \epsilon(\sigma)\leq\epsilon\}.

The per-iteration log-momentAJ,λ,m​(q)A_{J,\lambda,m}(q)decreases asσ\sigmagrows, i.e.,ϵ​(σ)\epsilon(\sigma)decreases monotonically inσ\sigma. The feasible set{σ:ϵ​(σ)≤ϵ}\{\sigma:\epsilon(\sigma)\leq\epsilon\}is then a half-line[σ⋆,∞)[\sigma^{\star},\infty), andσ⋆\sigma^{\star}is its unique boundary. We find it by a bisection scheme onlog⁡σ\log\sigma. The root is first bracketed by doublingσhi\sigma_{\mathrm{hi}}untilϵ​(σhi)≤ϵ\epsilon(\sigma_{\mathrm{hi}})\leq\epsilonand halvingσlo\sigma_{\mathrm{lo}}untilϵ​(σlo)>ϵ\epsilon(\sigma_{\mathrm{lo}})>\epsilon. The geometric midpointσmid=σlo​σhi\sigma_{\mathrm{mid}}=\sqrt{\sigma_{\mathrm{lo}}\sigma_{\mathrm{hi}}}then replacesσlo\sigma_{\mathrm{lo}}when it is infeasible andσhi\sigma_{\mathrm{hi}}otherwise, until the relative width(σhi−σlo)/σhi(\sigma_{\mathrm{hi}}-\sigma_{\mathrm{lo}})/\sigma_{\mathrm{hi}}falls below a tolerance. The feasible endpoint is returned, so the releasedσ\sigmaalways satisfiesϵ​(σ)≤ϵ\epsilon(\sigma)\leq\epsilonand the guarantee is conservative. The same routine applies to the choice ofσ\sigmafor DP-SGLD, which shares the replace-one Poisson-subsampled Gaussian accountant.

## S2Proof

## S2.1Proof of Proposition4.1and Theorem4.1

Throughout, (A1-5) hold. All constants depend only on the population quantitiesμ∞,dθ,dx\mu_{\infty},d_{\theta},d_{x}and the uniform derivative bounds ofllfrom (A3), never onnnorSS. We develop the common machinery first (SectionS2.1.1), then prove the Proposition (SectionS2.1.2) and the Theorem (SectionS2.1.3).

Letθ0⋆:=arg​minθ⁡L​(θ;n−1​𝟏n)\theta_{0}^{\star}:=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\theta}L(\theta;n^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{n})andθ∞⋆:=arg​minθ⁡𝔼​l​(X,θ)\theta_{\infty}^{\star}:=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\theta}{\mathbb{E}}\,l(X,\theta), and setH0,n:=H​(θ0⋆;n−1​𝟏n)H_{0,n}:=H(\theta_{0}^{\star};n^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{n}). together with the moment summariesΣψ,n:=1n​∑iψi​ψi⊤,Σ¯ψ,n:=1S​∑sψ¯s​ψ¯s⊤,σψ,n2:=Tr​Σψ,n,σ¯ψ,n2:=Tr​Σ¯ψ,n.\displaystyle\Sigma_{\psi,n}:=\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\psi_{i}\psi_{i}^{\top},\quad\bar{\Sigma}_{\psi,n}:=\tfrac{1}{S}\sum_{s}\bar{\psi}_{s}\bar{\psi}_{s}^{\top},\quad\sigma_{\psi,n}^{2}:=\text{Tr}\Sigma_{\psi,n},\quad\bar{\sigma}_{\psi,n}^{2}:=\text{Tr}\bar{\Sigma}_{\psi,n}.
